# Sociology click-by-click end-to-end test packages

**Purpose:** Run each package independently from its named test account, through the saved route, and into report printing or download. One package equals one student account; never carry selections from another package forward. Each package names the exact programme, institution, subject combination, method/design, area, task focus, role, items and tools to select. The generated route inventory states what should appear immediately after each selection.

## Click-by-click checks required in every package

Follow these steps in order. Each package below gives that student's exact subjects, institution, method/design, area, item, tool and role choices.

1. **Match the programme.** Select the package's student stage and enter every subject written in that package by its full displayed name. Do not substitute a generic “other subject” or carry a subject over from another package. SS1/SS2 use the stated school stream and O-level subject choices only; do not enter UTME. SS3 uses the exact O-level and UTME subjects listed in that package. Confirm the exact programme title **Sociology**—not Sociology and Social Anthropology or another programme. A selected subject is not proof of a grade; use the grade written in that package where the matcher asks for results.
2. **Complete the subject match before choosing a university.** For SS1, SS2, SS3 and UTME packages, first select the named subjects and submit them. The result list must show the exact **Sociology** programme before any university is named or selected. Open/select **Sociology**; only then choose the institution named in that package from the programme's university choices and inspect that institution's own requirement panel. Do not transfer another university's exception. The institution written in each package is the expected later choice, not a prefilled subject-matcher context. Undergraduate packages are the exception: choose university context in undergraduate setup and do not use the O-level/UTME matcher.
3. **Programme page.** The checkmark is on the same row as “Sociology”, at the far right. Tap it once: it becomes selected and the pinned tray immediately gains the programme and that package's institution context. Confirm the programme page shows themes with their own outcomes and attached resources; do not expect a duplicate outcome list or a placeholder resource button. Its Sociology-to-domain explanation distinguishes course learning from additional D078 learning and does not claim the degree teaches every domain item.
4. **Domain overview and pre-method foundations.** Open **Social Sciences & Behavioural Research**. Verify the overview and all three automatically selected compulsory foundations. Before touching a method, make a separate choice about **Society and the social world: relationships, cultures, communities and institutions**: each package must explicitly say whether to select it or leave it unselected. If selected, verify its tray row appears immediately; if skipped, verify it remains absent. It is useful, skippable field orientation—not a fourth compulsory foundation. The remaining method-dependent Layer 2 and Layer 3 choices must wait until a method/design is selected. The methods grid follows the foundations and orientation; there is no Continue button or acknowledgement gate.
5. **Select method(s) and combination design.** Only after completing the pre-method foundation choice, select the package's method or methods. After selecting one method, use **Add another method** only when the package asks for a combined route; compatible choices appear then, not before. For ordinary combinations, select the exact named design after selecting all its component methods. For participatory inquiry, select **Participatory and action research**, add the package's evidence method, then select the exact named design **Choose evidence methods with participants**. That design replaces the separate method cards and shows evidence-method choices inside it; any additional evidence method is selected there. The named design's selected component methods—not an automatic union of their separate routes—determine downstream choices. A participatory frame by itself is not a completed route.
6. **Complete the selected method's domain-learning stage before proceeding to an area.** After choosing the method or named combination, the method-linked domain items and tools must be presented before the area section in the page flow. Each package names (a) the exact route-linked domain item(s)/tool(s) to select, (b) at least one visible option to skip, and (c) any exact alternative pair to compare. If an item is not in that package's generated route menu, stop and record a mismatch; do not substitute a similarly named item from another route. Expand each named item with **See more**, review what it means and how it connects to the selected method/design, then tap **Add this** and confirm its tray row appears immediately. Complete the listed select/skip/compare checks before opening an area. These choices may be skippable; this sequence check does not turn every option into a progression blocker. If the area section appears before the method-learning section or a tester can open an area before completing this stage, record a display-flow failure.
7. **Area foundation, task-family focus, then area learning.** Open the package's named area only after step 6. The area's own foundation choices must come first. Each pack explicitly selects or skips the foundation it tests; where the graph records none, proceed directly to task-family choices. These foundations are selectable and skippable, not blockers. Next choose none, one, or the package's stated multiple task-family focus(es). Selecting none preserves the general area route; selecting a focus reveals only that focus's linked area items and the tools attached to those exact items. Do not repeat an item already selected as the area foundation. After the focus choice, select the exact area item(s)/tool(s) named by the package, skip another named option, and compare a graph-recorded alternative where specified. Check the separate card explanations, conditions, relationships and learning-resource rationale. Do not promote task-family membership into a prerequisite or treat tools as qualifications. Do not open a role until this area-stage check is complete. If the rendered UI does not show the area foundation before its task-family choices, or fails to reveal the focus-linked items and their attached tools after selection, record an acceptance failure.
8. **Check explanations and tools at each stage.** For domain, area, and role items, expand each named card with **See more**. Verify the explanation, why it matters in this context, how it relates to the chosen route, any output/limits, relationship details, and learning-link rationale are separated and understandable. Add only the named item; verify its tray row appears immediately. Select the named tool, inspect its purpose and route context, and inspect the named alternative without adding it. If replacing one tool with another, verify the old choice is removed. Tools are optional choices, not blockers or qualifications.
9. **Area-to-role transition and role items/tools.** In **Roles in this area for your selected methods**, verify the named role appears and open it from this area. The role page must retain the programme, institution, foundations, method/design, area, and earlier item/tool selections. Expand **From your route** and confirm it distinguishes selected domain learning from selected area learning before role-owned additions. Confirm role-owned learning items and **Tools by purpose** are separately selectable from area-owned items/tools. Add the exact role item and role tool named in this package. If a tool name also appeared in the area, verify its distinct role-specific explanation. Do not call a general role task “method-specific” unless the graph explicitly does so.
10. **Tray and saved route.** Verify the fixed tray updates at each selection without waiting for save and remains available while navigating the logged-in account until closed. A selected Society/social-world foundation or area foundation must appear in the tray; a skipped one must not. Confirm the window-style controls cycle collapsed → mid → large → mid, and minimize returns to collapsed. Delete a selected item only after the in-tray confirmation; cancel once to verify it remains, then confirm a deletion and verify it disappears. Save, open **My World**, and verify the saved route has this student's programme, institution context, Social Sciences, methods/design, selected areas, selected roles and only selected items/tools. Return to the route and verify unselected alternatives, methods, areas, roles, foundations and items are absent.
11. **Print/download.** Open this student's saved route and use **Print / download**. Confirm the output identifies the student's name and username, the attached university, the exact Sociology programme, Social Sciences, the selected method(s) and named design, selected area(s), task focus(es), role(s), and only the items/tools selected in this package. Use that package's **Tray / My World** line as the exact content checklist. Skipped choices and unselected alternatives must not appear. Sociology alone is not proof of a university affiliation; if the export reports a missing institution, stop and record the attachment issue instead of substituting another university.

**Exact programme:** Sociology (not Sociology and Social Anthropology). The downstream field is Social Sciences, D078. Test identities below are synthetic; create separate local test accounts as needed.

**Matcher evidence:** The current checked-in Sociology offering record lists **94 exact institutions**. Its national O-level rule is five credits including English Language and Government or History, plus three other subjects; Mathematics is a separate pass-only condition in that national rule. Its national UTME rule is three Arts or Social Science subjects (in addition to the fixed Use of English paper). Every package below names its actual subjects; a subject is never supplied by a vague “other subject” placeholder. Institution-specific requirements must be read from the selected Sociology institution’s own panel. Do not borrow a requirement from another university or another programme. These local seed/source records establish expected local data only; they do not prove that the same seed is live in Supabase.

## Coverage and expected common results

The 38 manual packages cover all 23 graph method nodes, all nine named combined designs, all seven canonical areas, and all 19 student-visible roles, including one multi-area package. They include no-focus, selected/skipped optional foundation, method-item, area-item and role-item choices; tool choices; graph-recorded alternatives; deselection/restoration; and report checks. The graph's 207 declared route plans are checked by local route-completeness tests; this document is the rendered click-through set, not a claim that a person must manually replay every mathematically possible optional-checkbox subset.

For every package:

1. The exact **Sociology** programme result is available for the stated subject set and stage. SS1/SS2 use O-level subjects only; SS3 uses O-level plus UTME. The result is guidance, not an admission decision.
2. For admission-subject packages, do not enter, display, or select the package's institution before the subject results and exact Sociology programme have appeared. Select Sociology first, then select the package's named institution from the offering list. It must be one of the 94 exact Sociology offerings in the checked-in record. The university's own requirement is reviewed after that selection; it is not a filter or label shown ahead of the subject results. Undergraduate packages carry their university context in the undergraduate setup without running the O-level/UTME matcher. Never substitute one institution’s rules for another.
3. The Sociology programme page shows the programme themes, each theme’s own outcomes and its attached resources where seeded. It then explains the reviewed Sociology-to-Social-Sciences connection; it must not claim the degree taught every D078 item.
4. Selecting **Social Sciences (D078)** shows its overview and compulsory foundations. Before a method is selected, method-dependent items/areas/roles are not presented as if chosen. After a method/design is selected, only graph-supported routes and choices are shown.
5. The tray starts with the programme and selected institution context, then adds D078, method/design, selected area, role, and only the individual items/tools selected. Each tap updates selection immediately; deselection removes that choice. Save and reopen through **My World**.
6. My World shows this student’s saved choices only, in programme → institution context → field → method/design → area → role → selected items/tools order. It does not add unselected alternatives.

## Route, requirement and skip rules for every pack

- The sequence is **programme and university context → domain overview → three compulsory foundations plus the explicit select/skip choice for the optional Society/social-world foundation → method or methods → exact named combination (when applicable) → method/design-linked domain items and tools → area overview and its own optional foundation (when one exists) → task-family choice(s) → linked area items/tools → role choice within that area → role-task-linked items/tools**. A package that omits a selection/skip action at one of these stages, or sends the student downstream before checking the prior stage, is incomplete and must not be used for acceptance testing.
- The graph does **not** create a different method/area/role route for every optional-item checkbox combination. The selected method (or exact named design) determines its domain-item choices and recommended areas; the chosen area then determines area items/tools and area-owned roles. Within those stages, selected items are a separate set of choices; adding or skipping a useful item must not change which graph-supported areas or roles are available unless an explicit dependency says so.
- The three domain foundations are the compulsory starting spine and remain carried into the selected area/role route. The society and social-world section follows them; the methods grid is already visible below it. There is no Continue button, review acknowledgement, or mastery gate before methods. Other useful, optional, or conditional items never block progression just because they were skipped.
- A route item marked **Needed** is scoped to the selected method/task. A **Conditional** item applies only when its stated condition applies. **Useful/Optional** items are skippable. The graph's current policy is non-blocking for these items unless a specific approved dependency explicitly says otherwise. Do not make every item a prerequisite or require every option in the tray.
- Test skipping: at the **domain-item stage**, select the package's named core/relevant item, explicitly leave its named useful/optional item unselected, and only then continue to the same graph-supported area and role. Repeat this at the area-item stage where the package names a skippable item. Test conditional items by recording the stated condition; do not add them when it does not apply. If a task makes a condition applicable, confirm the item is identified as needed for that task, but do not claim a completion blocker exists unless the UI actually implements a reviewed prerequisite and completion state.
- Test alternatives: compare the exact alternative pair named by that pack. Comparison alone must not change the tray. If the UI offers **Use this instead**, confirm the replacement and verify the prior choice is removed and the replacement is present. If the UI instead permits both to remain, record that as a selection-contract defect; do not describe the two as mutually exclusive unless the graph defines them as a choose-one group.
- After each tap, verify the immediate state: selected card/checkmark, tray row added or removed, route unchanged for an item-only change, and no unrelated items auto-added. In a multi-method pack, selecting methods alone is not enough: choose the exact named design before judging its areas, roles, items or tools.

## Exact subject-entry instructions

Enter the full names written in each package in the subject picker, not codes. The following are reference lists only; each SS3 package repeats its own complete choices and grades so it can be tested independently.

- **O-level fixture, Government route:** English Language (credit); Government (credit); Mathematics (pass); Economics (credit); Christian Religious Studies (credit); Biology (credit). The five credits are English Language, Government and the three named additional subjects; Mathematics is a separate pass in the national rule.
- **O-level fixture, History route:** English Language (credit); History (credit); Mathematics (pass); Geography (credit); Literature in English (credit); Commerce (credit). The five credits are English Language, History and the three named additional subjects; Mathematics is a separate pass in the national rule.
- **SS1 Art subjects:** English Language; Mathematics; Government; Christian Religious Studies; Literature in English; Economics. These are the exact choices for Package 01; Biology is not part of this student's Art-stream choices.
- **SS2 Commercial subjects:** English Language; Mathematics; Government; Economics; Commerce; Geography. These are the exact choices for Package 02; History and Literature in English are not part of this student's Commercial-stream choices.
- **UTME fixture, Government route:** Use of English (fixed); Government; Economics; History. The three selected UTME subjects are Government, Economics and History.
- **UTME fixture, Arts route:** Use of English (fixed); Literature in English; Geography; Christian Religious Studies. The three selected UTME subjects are Literature in English, Geography and Christian Religious Studies.

For SS1/SS2, each pack lists the exact stream-specific subjects; do not add UTME or infer grades. These are matcher-preview journeys, not claims that the student already holds O-level results. For SS3, enter the exact O-level and UTME subjects repeated in that pack, with its stated grades. The two UTME fixtures each contain three named Arts/Social Science subjects plus the fixed Use of English paper.

The package sections above identify the student and give the exact matcher/account setup. This generated section is the sole authority for D078 click actions; it replaces older D078 prose so no pack can send the student downstream early. The graph contract is validated for every route before this document is written. Required screen order: three already-selected compulsory domain foundations → the optional Society and the social world choice (select or skip) → method or methods → the exact named design when supported → that route's domain learning and tools → selected area and its optional foundation → task-family choice(s) → only the selected focus's area learning and attached tools → role within that area → only reviewed role-task-linked learning/tools → tray, save, My World and print. Optional choices stay optional. Within a stage, use only explicit reviewed sequence edges; otherwise choices are parallel, not alphabetically ordered or guessed.

### Pack 1: exact screen-by-screen route and selections

**Route key:** `method-area-route:d078-quantitative-approach:economics-applied-analysis`

1. **Social Sciences domain entry.** Confirm these three compulsory foundation cards are already selected:
- Frame a researchable social question and choose a fitting design
- Evaluate evidence and explain what it can and cannot show
- Communicate findings for the intended audience
2. **Optional Society choice, before any method appears or is selected.** This is a separate selectable orientation between the compulsory foundations and the method grid:
- Society and the social world: relationships, cultures, communities and institutions
**Pack 1 pre-method action:** select **Society and the social world: relationships, cultures, communities and institutions**. Then verify that the methods are available either way. If selected, its tray row appears immediately; if skipped, no row appears and the next stage is still methods. It is never a fourth compulsory foundation.

3. **Method/design choice, only after the Society select-or-skip check.** Select these exact method card(s):

This is a single-method route. The method remains usable on its own; a combined design should appear only if the learner adds a compatible method and the graph has an exact supported design for that selected set.

4. **After method/design selection and before opening an area: domain learning and tools.** Only the groups and cards in this exact route menu should appear. For every group, perform the generated select/skip/compare actions immediately below its menu; no area is opened until this stage is checked.

- Find and assess existing social data — useful and optional

- **Route context:** Turning ideas into observable measures is linked to Quantitative methods (working with numbers).
- **Route context:** Finding and organising research sources is linked to Quantitative methods (working with numbers).

**Research practices and conditional preparation (Layer 3; optional, route-linked choices):**
_Useful and optional:_
- Plan research responsibly when people or sensitive data are involved — useful edge; condition: Choose this preparation when the selected research task will involve people or identifiable/sensitive information. Use it before collecting or using that information to identify consent, privacy, possible harm and the permissions or review that apply.
- Organise and protect research information when a project uses data — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Use this when a project creates, receives, stores or shares research information and needs to document what it contains, protect it and control access.
- Keeping a clear, repeatable research record — useful and optional
- Ethics for AI-assisted and digital-trace research — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Assess human oversight, data quality, provenance, consent, privacy, bias and disclosure when AI or online traces are used.
- **Route context:** Ethics for AI-assisted and digital-trace research is linked to Quantitative methods (working with numbers).
- **Route context:** Organise and protect research information when a project uses data is linked to Quantitative methods (working with numbers).
- **Route context:** Plan research responsibly when people or sensitive data are involved is linked to Quantitative methods (working with numbers).
- **Route context:** Keeping a clear, repeatable research record is linked to Quantitative methods (working with numbers).
- This group is skippable; its placement organizes the route and does not make it a prerequisite.
**Method-linked choices: Quantitative methods (working with numbers):**
_Useful and optional:_
- Sensitivity and robustness analysis for quantitative findings — useful edge; condition: Use sensitivity or robustness checks when a quantitative result depends on uncertain inputs, modelling choices or assumptions that could materially change the conclusion.
- Longitudinal, panel and multilevel data — useful and optional
- Use AI cautiously to assist cross-source social-data harmonisation — optional; condition: Experimental optional support for comparing existing datasets. Verify every proposed mapping against source codebooks, populations, measures, periods, and transformation rules.
- Use AI to draft or improve research-data metadata, then curate and verify it — optional; condition: Optional experimental support for drafting or reviewing metadata. A human curator checks every field against the source and repository schema.
- Validate and document AI use in a research workflow — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Use only when AI is used in the selected workflow; record the system, version, task, data context, checks, and required disclosure.
- ANOVA: group, factorial and repeated-measures comparisons — useful and optional
- Barnard's unconditional exact test for 2×2 tables — useful and optional
- Boschloo's unconditional exact test for 2×2 tables — useful and optional
- Chi-square tests for categorical counts and association — useful and optional
- Confirmatory factor analysis (CFA) — useful and optional
- Count regression: Poisson and negative binomial models — useful and optional
- Exploratory factor analysis (EFA) — useful and optional
- Exponential-family random graph models (ERGMs) — useful and optional
- Fisher's exact test for categorical counts — useful and optional
- Friedman rank test for matched or blocked groups — useful and optional
- Item response theory (IRT) and differential item functioning — useful and optional
- Kendall rank correlation — useful and optional
- Kruskal–Wallis rank test for independent groups — useful and optional
- Simple and multiple linear regression (ordinary least squares) — useful and optional
- Logistic regression for binary outcomes — useful and optional
- Machine learning for survey nonresponse adjustment — useful and optional
- Multinomial logistic regression for unordered categorical outcomes — useful and optional
- Ordinal regression for ordered categorical outcomes — useful and optional
- Pearson product-moment correlation — useful and optional
- Probit regression for binary outcomes — useful and optional
- Regularized linear regression: ridge, LASSO and elastic net — useful and optional
- Scale reliability estimation: alpha, omega and alternatives — useful and optional
- Spatial autocorrelation: Moran's I and local indicators (LISA) — useful and optional
- Spatial regression: spatial lag and spatial error models — useful and optional
- Spearman rank correlation — useful and optional
- Supervised machine learning for social-research prediction and classification — useful and optional
- One-sample, independent-samples and paired t-tests — useful and optional
- Tree-ensemble machine learning for prediction and classification — useful and optional
- Wilcoxon rank-sum and signed-rank tests — useful and optional
- Quantitative methods (working with numbers) — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Choose this approach when a question needs numerical measurement, counting, comparison or a statistical summary; it is an option, not a requirement for every route.
_Tools attached to this method contribution; compare and select only what you plan to use:_
- Power BI for social-research reporting and interactive dashboards
- Tableau for social-research visualisation and dashboards
- EViews for econometric time-series analysis
- Jamovi for introductory quantitative social-science analysis
- JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis
- Jupyter
- Python
- Qualtrics for online survey design and response management
- R mirt package for item response theory
- R psych package for reliability and factor analysis
- SAS/STAT for statistical modelling and survey analysis
- Scikit-learn
- Statnet ergm for social network models
- Statsmodels
- Stata for quantitative social-science workflows
- R for statistical computing and data analysis
- SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it
- This group is skippable; its placement organizes the route and does not make it a prerequisite.

8. **Alternatives and order checks:**
- Compare **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts** with **Chi-square tests for categorical counts and association**: For sparse categorical-count tables, choose the exact or justified resampling procedure when the large-sample chi-square approximation is unsuitable. The tests have different reference distributions and sampling assumptions.
- Compare **Barnard's unconditional exact test for 2×2 tables** with **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts**: For independent-binomial 2×2 data, Barnard's unconditional test is an exact alternative when conditioning on both margins is not required. Its an extra unknown value the test has to account for method, variance choice and sampling assumptions differ from Fisher's conditional exact test.
- Compare **Boschloo's unconditional exact test for 2×2 tables** with **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts**: For independent-binomial 2×2 data, Boschloo's unconditional test is an exact alternative based on Fisher's p-value and may be more powerful. State the sampling assumptions and two-sided p-value convention.
- Compare **Wilcoxon rank-sum and signed-rank tests** with **One-sample, independent-samples and paired t-tests**: For a two-group independent or paired contrast, a rank-based procedure may fit the outcome/design better. It targets a rank/distribution contrast and is not a drop-in mean test.
- Compare **Kruskal–Wallis rank test for independent groups** with **ANOVA: group, factorial and repeated-measures comparisons**: For independent groups, a rank-based overall alternative may fit when the inferential target is a distribution/location contrast rather than group means. Use a design-matched model when covariates or complex structure matter.
- Compare **Friedman rank test for matched or blocked groups** with **ANOVA: group, factorial and repeated-measures comparisons**: For complete matched blocks with one observation per condition, a rank-based repeated comparison can be an alternative to an appropriate repeated-measures ANOVA. It does not handle every unbalanced or incomplete design.
- Compare **Probit regression for binary outcomes** with **Logistic regression for binary outcomes**: Probit and logit are alternative link functions for a binomial model of a binary outcome. Compare fit and predicted probabilities/marginal effects rather than raw coefficient scales.
- Compare **Tree-ensemble machine learning for prediction and classification** with **Logistic regression for binary outcomes**: For a prediction/classification goal, tree ensembles can compete with logistic regression as predictive models. Logistic regression remains a distinct inferential GLM and neither model establishes a causal effect.
- Compare **Spearman rank correlation** with **Pearson product-moment correlation**: For the same association question, use Spearman for ordinal/rank or monotonic association where Pearson's linear quantitative association is not the target. Related but distinct coefficients answer different questions.
- Compare **Kendall rank correlation** with **Pearson product-moment correlation**: For the same association question, Kendall is a rank-based alternative when concordance, ordinal measurement or tie handling better fits than Pearson's linear association.
- Compare **Kendall rank correlation** with **Spearman rank correlation**: Choose between rank coefficients by the result you set out to estimate, tie pattern, sample and inferential procedure; Kendall tau and Spearman rho are related but not numerically interchangeable.
- Compare **Regularized linear regression: ridge, LASSO and elastic net** with **Simple and multiple linear regression (ordinary least squares)**: For high-dimensional or prediction-focused work with collinearity, penalized regression can stabilize or select coefficients relative to OLS. Shrinkage changes estimation and interpretation, so this is a conditional specialist extension rather than a default replacement.
- Compare **Tree-ensemble machine learning for prediction and classification** with **Simple and multiple linear regression (ordinary least squares)**: For continuous-outcome prediction, compare nonlinear tree ensembles with a linear baseline on kept-back test data data. The choice is goal- and validation-dependent and neither model is causal by itself.
- Compare **Count regression: Poisson and negative binomial models** with **Supervised machine learning for social-research prediction and classification**: Count regression provides an outcome-distribution-based inferential model; ML regressors may be compared for prediction but are not direct substitutes for its rate interpretation or inferential the result you set out to estimate.
- Compare **Item response theory (IRT) and differential item functioning** with **Confirmatory factor analysis (CFA)**: IRT and CFA can address measurement structure and comparability through different model frameworks. Choose according to item response format, the result you set out to estimate, sample and assumptions rather than treating scores as equivalent.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SAS/STAT for statistical modelling and survey analysis**: Both document common inferential procedures; choose based on the exact model, access and reproducibility needs.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **Jamovi for introductory quantitative social-science analysis**: Both provide graphical workflows for common statistical procedures; choose based on analysis coverage, reproducibility and access.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: Both support common introductory tests and models; procedures and licensing differ, so compare the required analysis.

The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.

- Case-study and comparative-case design — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Set defensible boundaries around a case and compare cases without treating one example as universal.

8. **Alternatives and order checks:**
- Compare **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts** with **Chi-square tests for categorical counts and association**: For sparse categorical-count tables, choose the exact or justified resampling procedure when the large-sample chi-square approximation is unsuitable. The tests have different reference distributions and sampling assumptions.
- Compare **Barnard's unconditional exact test for 2×2 tables** with **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts**: For independent-binomial 2×2 data, Barnard's unconditional test is an exact alternative when conditioning on both margins is not required. Its an extra unknown value the test has to account for method, variance choice and sampling assumptions differ from Fisher's conditional exact test.
- Compare **Boschloo's unconditional exact test for 2×2 tables** with **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts**: For independent-binomial 2×2 data, Boschloo's unconditional test is an exact alternative based on Fisher's p-value and may be more powerful. State the sampling assumptions and two-sided p-value convention.
- Compare **Wilcoxon rank-sum and signed-rank tests** with **One-sample, independent-samples and paired t-tests**: For a two-group independent or paired contrast, a rank-based procedure may fit the outcome/design better. It targets a rank/distribution contrast and is not a drop-in mean test.
- Compare **Spearman rank correlation** with **Pearson product-moment correlation**: For the same association question, use Spearman for ordinal/rank or monotonic association where Pearson's linear quantitative association is not the target. Related but distinct coefficients answer different questions.
- Compare **Kendall rank correlation** with **Pearson product-moment correlation**: For the same association question, Kendall is a rank-based alternative when concordance, ordinal measurement or tie handling better fits than Pearson's linear association.
- Compare **Kendall rank correlation** with **Spearman rank correlation**: Choose between rank coefficients by the result you set out to estimate, tie pattern, sample and inferential procedure; Kendall tau and Spearman rho are related but not numerically interchangeable.
- Compare **Item response theory (IRT) and differential item functioning** with **Confirmatory factor analysis (CFA)**: IRT and CFA can address measurement structure and comparability through different model frameworks. Choose according to item response format, the result you set out to estimate, sample and assumptions rather than treating scores as equivalent.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SAS/STAT for statistical modelling and survey analysis**: Both document common inferential procedures; choose based on the exact model, access and reproducibility needs.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **Jamovi for introductory quantitative social-science analysis**: Both provide graphical workflows for common statistical procedures; choose based on analysis coverage, reproducibility and access.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: Both support common introductory tests and models; procedures and licensing differ, so compare the required analysis.
- Compare **Qualtrics for online survey design and response management** with **ODK for offline-capable field data collection**: Both can implement digital questionnaires; online panel workflows and offline field collection have different strengths.
- Compare **Microsoft Excel** with **R for statistical computing and data analysis**: For this role task, R for statistical computing is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Microsoft Excel** with **Stata for quantitative social-science workflows**: For this role task, Stata is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Stata for quantitative social-science workflows** with **R for statistical computing and data analysis**: For this role task, R for statistical computing is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it** with **R for statistical computing and data analysis**: For this role task, R for statistical computing is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Stata for quantitative social-science workflows** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: For this role task, IBM SPSS Statistics is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Microsoft Excel** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: For this role task, IBM SPSS Statistics is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Surveys and questionnaire design** with **Find and assess existing social data**: A survey, interview, observation or existing-data route may answer a question using different evidence. Choose by the question and data needed; these routes can also be combined when the design explains why.
- Follow these reviewed route order relationships:
- Population measures and demographic change → Interpret migration and urbanisation patterns (workflow; Use this order for demographic questions where measures are needed to interpret migration, urbanisation or population change; it is not required for every human-geography task.)
- Appraise census, survey and administrative population data → Population measures and demographic change (workflow; When deriving a population measure from a census, survey, or administrative source, first check how that source was collected, defined, and covered. This is a task workflow, not a requirement to take a separate course first.)

8. **Alternatives and order checks:**
- Compare **MAXQDA for qualitative and mixed-methods analysis** with **ATLAS.ti for qualitative data coding and retrieval**: Both support organizing, coding and retrieving qualitative material; compare workflow and access needs.
- Compare **MAXQDA for qualitative and mixed-methods analysis** with **QualCoder for open-source qualitative analysis**: Both support qualitative coding and retrieval; one is commercial and one is open source.
- Compare **ATLAS.ti for qualitative data coding and retrieval** with **Dedoose for collaborative qualitative and mixed-methods analysis**: Both can support coded qualitative projects; collaboration and mixed-method needs may influence the choice.

8. **Alternatives and order checks:**
- Compare **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts** with **Chi-square tests for categorical counts and association**: For sparse categorical-count tables, choose the exact or justified resampling procedure when the large-sample chi-square approximation is unsuitable. The tests have different reference distributions and sampling assumptions.
- Compare **Barnard's unconditional exact test for 2×2 tables** with **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts**: For independent-binomial 2×2 data, Barnard's unconditional test is an exact alternative when conditioning on both margins is not required. Its an extra unknown value the test has to account for method, variance choice and sampling assumptions differ from Fisher's conditional exact test.
- Compare **Boschloo's unconditional exact test for 2×2 tables** with **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts**: For independent-binomial 2×2 data, Boschloo's unconditional test is an exact alternative based on Fisher's p-value and may be more powerful. State the sampling assumptions and two-sided p-value convention.
- Compare **Wilcoxon rank-sum and signed-rank tests** with **One-sample, independent-samples and paired t-tests**: For a two-group independent or paired contrast, a rank-based procedure may fit the outcome/design better. It targets a rank/distribution contrast and is not a drop-in mean test.
- Compare **Spearman rank correlation** with **Pearson product-moment correlation**: For the same association question, use Spearman for ordinal/rank or monotonic association where Pearson's linear quantitative association is not the target. Related but distinct coefficients answer different questions.
- Compare **Kendall rank correlation** with **Pearson product-moment correlation**: For the same association question, Kendall is a rank-based alternative when concordance, ordinal measurement or tie handling better fits than Pearson's linear association.
- Compare **Kendall rank correlation** with **Spearman rank correlation**: Choose between rank coefficients by the result you set out to estimate, tie pattern, sample and inferential procedure; Kendall tau and Spearman rho are related but not numerically interchangeable.
- Compare **Item response theory (IRT) and differential item functioning** with **Confirmatory factor analysis (CFA)**: IRT and CFA can address measurement structure and comparability through different model frameworks. Choose according to item response format, the result you set out to estimate, sample and assumptions rather than treating scores as equivalent.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SAS/STAT for statistical modelling and survey analysis**: Both document common inferential procedures; choose based on the exact model, access and reproducibility needs.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **Jamovi for introductory quantitative social-science analysis**: Both provide graphical workflows for common statistical procedures; choose based on analysis coverage, reproducibility and access.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: Both support common introductory tests and models; procedures and licensing differ, so compare the required analysis.
- Compare **MAXQDA for qualitative and mixed-methods analysis** with **ATLAS.ti for qualitative data coding and retrieval**: Both support organizing, coding and retrieving qualitative material; compare workflow and access needs.
- Compare **MAXQDA for qualitative and mixed-methods analysis** with **QualCoder for open-source qualitative analysis**: Both support qualitative coding and retrieval; one is commercial and one is open source.
- Compare **ATLAS.ti for qualitative data coding and retrieval** with **Dedoose for collaborative qualitative and mixed-methods analysis**: Both can support coded qualitative projects; collaboration and mixed-method needs may influence the choice.
- Compare **QualCoder for open-source qualitative analysis** with **Taguette for highlighting and coding documents**: Both support document coding workflows; choose according to collaboration, data types and installation needs.
- Compare **Qualtrics for online survey design and response management** with **ODK for offline-capable field data collection**: Both can implement digital questionnaires; online panel workflows and offline field collection have different strengths.
- Compare **Microsoft Excel** with **R for statistical computing and data analysis**: For this role task, R for statistical computing is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.

8. **Alternatives and order checks:**
- Compare **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts** with **Chi-square tests for categorical counts and association**: For sparse categorical-count tables, choose the exact or justified resampling procedure when the large-sample chi-square approximation is unsuitable. The tests have different reference distributions and sampling assumptions.
- Compare **Barnard's unconditional exact test for 2×2 tables** with **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts**: For independent-binomial 2×2 data, Barnard's unconditional test is an exact alternative when conditioning on both margins is not required. Its an extra unknown value the test has to account for method, variance choice and sampling assumptions differ from Fisher's conditional exact test.
- Compare **Boschloo's unconditional exact test for 2×2 tables** with **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts**: For independent-binomial 2×2 data, Boschloo's unconditional test is an exact alternative based on Fisher's p-value and may be more powerful. State the sampling assumptions and two-sided p-value convention.
- Compare **Wilcoxon rank-sum and signed-rank tests** with **One-sample, independent-samples and paired t-tests**: For a two-group independent or paired contrast, a rank-based procedure may fit the outcome/design better. It targets a rank/distribution contrast and is not a drop-in mean test.
- Compare **Spearman rank correlation** with **Pearson product-moment correlation**: For the same association question, use Spearman for ordinal/rank or monotonic association where Pearson's linear quantitative association is not the target. Related but distinct coefficients answer different questions.
- Compare **Kendall rank correlation** with **Pearson product-moment correlation**: For the same association question, Kendall is a rank-based alternative when concordance, ordinal measurement or tie handling better fits than Pearson's linear association.
- Compare **Kendall rank correlation** with **Spearman rank correlation**: Choose between rank coefficients by the result you set out to estimate, tie pattern, sample and inferential procedure; Kendall tau and Spearman rho are related but not numerically interchangeable.
- Compare **Item response theory (IRT) and differential item functioning** with **Confirmatory factor analysis (CFA)**: IRT and CFA can address measurement structure and comparability through different model frameworks. Choose according to item response format, the result you set out to estimate, sample and assumptions rather than treating scores as equivalent.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SAS/STAT for statistical modelling and survey analysis**: Both document common inferential procedures; choose based on the exact model, access and reproducibility needs.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **Jamovi for introductory quantitative social-science analysis**: Both provide graphical workflows for common statistical procedures; choose based on analysis coverage, reproducibility and access.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: Both support common introductory tests and models; procedures and licensing differ, so compare the required analysis.
- Compare **MAXQDA for qualitative and mixed-methods analysis** with **ATLAS.ti for qualitative data coding and retrieval**: Both support organizing, coding and retrieving qualitative material; compare workflow and access needs.
- Compare **MAXQDA for qualitative and mixed-methods analysis** with **QualCoder for open-source qualitative analysis**: Both support qualitative coding and retrieval; one is commercial and one is open source.
- Compare **ATLAS.ti for qualitative data coding and retrieval** with **Dedoose for collaborative qualitative and mixed-methods analysis**: Both can support coded qualitative projects; collaboration and mixed-method needs may influence the choice.
- Compare **QualCoder for open-source qualitative analysis** with **Taguette for highlighting and coding documents**: Both support document coding workflows; choose according to collaboration, data types and installation needs.
- Compare **Qualtrics for online survey design and response management** with **ODK for offline-capable field data collection**: Both can implement digital questionnaires; online panel workflows and offline field collection have different strengths.
- Compare **PsychoPy** with **OpenSesame**: Both build behavioural experiments; compare required components, online runtime and hardware support.
- Compare **PsychoPy** with **PsyToolkit**: Both support behavioural experiment workflows; compare the required design and online/offline constraints.
- Compare **NVivo for organizing and coding substantial qualitative material** with **Taguette for highlighting and coding documents**: For this role task, Taguette is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Stata for quantitative social-science workflows** with **R for statistical computing and data analysis**: For this role task, R for statistical computing is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it** with **R for statistical computing and data analysis**: For this role task, R for statistical computing is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **OpenSesame** with **PsyToolkit**: For this role task, PsyToolkit is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Stata for quantitative social-science workflows** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: For this role task, IBM SPSS Statistics is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Surveys and questionnaire design** with **Interviews**: A survey, interview, observation or existing-data route may answer a question using different evidence. Choose by the question and data needed; these routes can also be combined when the design explains why.
- No reviewed relative-order edge is recorded among these route choices. They remain parallel/skippable; do not impose an alphabetical or guessed order.

**Method-linked choices: Spatial analysis and mapping with suitable social data:**
_Useful and optional:_
- Archival and historical research — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Use records and archives as evidence while checking provenance, context and gaps.
- Case-study and comparative-case design — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Set defensible boundaries around a case and compare cases without treating one example as universal.
- Longitudinal, panel and multilevel data — useful and optional
- Evidence synthesis: systematic reviews and scoping reviews — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Find, screen, assess and combine existing studies rather than collecting new data by default.
- Use AI cautiously to assist cross-source social-data harmonisation — optional; condition: Experimental optional support for comparing existing datasets. Verify every proposed mapping against source codebooks, populations, measures, periods, and transformation rules.
- Use AI to draft or improve research-data metadata, then curate and verify it — optional; condition: Optional experimental support for drafting or reviewing metadata. A human curator checks every field against the source and repository schema.
- Spatial autocorrelation: Moran's I and local indicators (LISA) — useful and optional
- Spatial regression: spatial lag and spatial error models — useful and optional

8. **Alternatives and order checks:**
- Compare **Wilcoxon rank-sum and signed-rank tests** with **One-sample, independent-samples and paired t-tests**: For a two-group independent or paired contrast, a rank-based procedure may fit the outcome/design better. It targets a rank/distribution contrast and is not a drop-in mean test.
- Compare **Kruskal–Wallis rank test for independent groups** with **ANOVA: group, factorial and repeated-measures comparisons**: For independent groups, a rank-based overall alternative may fit when the inferential target is a distribution/location contrast rather than group means. Use a design-matched model when covariates or complex structure matter.
- Compare **Friedman rank test for matched or blocked groups** with **ANOVA: group, factorial and repeated-measures comparisons**: For complete matched blocks with one observation per condition, a rank-based repeated comparison can be an alternative to an appropriate repeated-measures ANOVA. It does not handle every unbalanced or incomplete design.
- Compare **Probit regression for binary outcomes** with **Logistic regression for binary outcomes**: Probit and logit are alternative link functions for a binomial model of a binary outcome. Compare fit and predicted probabilities/marginal effects rather than raw coefficient scales.
- Compare **Regularized linear regression: ridge, LASSO and elastic net** with **Simple and multiple linear regression (ordinary least squares)**: For high-dimensional or prediction-focused work with collinearity, penalized regression can stabilize or select coefficients relative to OLS. Shrinkage changes estimation and interpretation, so this is a conditional specialist extension rather than a default replacement.
- Compare **Count regression: Poisson and negative binomial models** with **Supervised machine learning for social-research prediction and classification**: Count regression provides an outcome-distribution-based inferential model; ML regressors may be compared for prediction but are not direct substitutes for its rate interpretation or inferential the result you set out to estimate.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SAS/STAT for statistical modelling and survey analysis**: Both document common inferential procedures; choose based on the exact model, access and reproducibility needs.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **Jamovi for introductory quantitative social-science analysis**: Both provide graphical workflows for common statistical procedures; choose based on analysis coverage, reproducibility and access.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: Both support common introductory tests and models; procedures and licensing differ, so compare the required analysis.
- Compare **Stata for quantitative social-science workflows** with **R for statistical computing and data analysis**: For this role task, R for statistical computing is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Pandas for cleaning and combining tabular research data** with **Stata for quantitative social-science workflows**: For this role task, Stata is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Pandas for cleaning and combining tabular research data** with **R for statistical computing and data analysis**: For this role task, R for statistical computing is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it** with **R for statistical computing and data analysis**: For this role task, R for statistical computing is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Stata for quantitative social-science workflows** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: For this role task, IBM SPSS Statistics is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Pandas for cleaning and combining tabular research data** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: For this role task, IBM SPSS Statistics is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Follow these reviewed route order relationships:
- Turn a social question into a feasible study design → Sampling, measurement and representativeness (workflow; Frame the research question before deciding what sample and measures can answer it; these activities can be learned together.)
- Describe how a programme is expected to produce change → Select evaluation questions, design and credible evidence (workflow; Use this order when the evaluation uses a programme theory or logic model; a descriptive review may not need one.)

Skip this branch when quantitative analysis is not suitable.)

- Sensitivity and robustness analysis for quantitative findings — useful edge; condition: Use sensitivity or robustness checks when a quantitative result depends on uncertain inputs, modelling choices or assumptions that could materially change the conclusion.
- Scenario analysis across plausible futures — useful edge; condition: Use scenario analysis when the question is about how a policy, programme or social system could respond to several plausible future conditions, rather than predicting one certain future.

- Evidence synthesis: systematic reviews and scoping reviews — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Find, screen, assess and combine existing studies rather than collecting new data by default.
- Investigate how AI systems affect people, groups and institutions — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Optional when the research question concerns an AI system's effects on people, groups, or institutions; scope the assessment and involve affected stakeholders where appropriate.
- Count regression: Poisson and negative binomial models — useful and optional
- Simple and multiple linear regression (ordinary least squares) — useful and optional
- Logistic regression for binary outcomes — useful and optional
- Multinomial logistic regression for unordered categorical outcomes — useful and optional
- Ordinal regression for ordered categorical outcomes — useful and optional
- Probit regression for binary outcomes — useful and optional
- Process tracing for within-case causal mechanisms — useful and optional
- One-sample, independent-samples and paired t-tests — useful and optional

8. **Alternatives and order checks:**
- Compare **Spearman rank correlation** with **Pearson product-moment correlation**: For the same association question, use Spearman for ordinal/rank or monotonic association where Pearson's linear quantitative association is not the target. Related but distinct coefficients answer different questions.
- Compare **Kendall rank correlation** with **Pearson product-moment correlation**: For the same association question, Kendall is a rank-based alternative when concordance, ordinal measurement or tie handling better fits than Pearson's linear association.
- Compare **Kendall rank correlation** with **Spearman rank correlation**: Choose between rank coefficients by the result you set out to estimate, tie pattern, sample and inferential procedure; Kendall tau and Spearman rho are related but not numerically interchangeable.
- Compare **Item response theory (IRT) and differential item functioning** with **Confirmatory factor analysis (CFA)**: IRT and CFA can address measurement structure and comparability through different model frameworks. Choose according to item response format, the result you set out to estimate, sample and assumptions rather than treating scores as equivalent.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SAS/STAT for statistical modelling and survey analysis**: Both document common inferential procedures; choose based on the exact model, access and reproducibility needs.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **Jamovi for introductory quantitative social-science analysis**: Both provide graphical workflows for common statistical procedures; choose based on analysis coverage, reproducibility and access.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: Both support common introductory tests and models; procedures and licensing differ, so compare the required analysis.
- Compare **PsychoPy** with **OpenSesame**: Both build behavioural experiments; compare required components, online runtime and hardware support.
- Compare **PsychoPy** with **PsyToolkit**: Both support behavioural experiment workflows; compare the required design and online/offline constraints.
- Compare **Stata for quantitative social-science workflows** with **R for statistical computing and data analysis**: For this role task, R for statistical computing is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it** with **R for statistical computing and data analysis**: For this role task, R for statistical computing is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **OpenSesame** with **PsyToolkit**: For this role task, PsyToolkit is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **Stata for quantitative social-science workflows** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: For this role task, IBM SPSS Statistics is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- No reviewed relative-order edge is recorded among these route choices. They remain parallel/skippable; do not impose an alphabetical or guessed order.
- Deselecting any optional item/tool removes it from the route and saved selection; it must not block the next stage. Returning to an earlier stage must preserve other still-selected choices.

_Useful and optional:_
- Plan research responsibly when people or sensitive data are involved — useful edge; condition: Choose this preparation when the selected research task will involve people or identifiable/sensitive information. Use it before collecting or using that information to identify consent, privacy, possible harm and the permissions or review that apply.
- Organise and protect research information when a project uses data — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Use this when a project creates, receives, stores or shares research information and needs to document what it contains, protect it and control access.

**Method-linked choices: Computational and digital social-research methods:**
_Useful and optional:_
- Archival and historical research — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Use records and archives as evidence while checking provenance, context and gaps.
- Case-study and comparative-case design — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Set defensible boundaries around a case and compare cases without treating one example as universal.
- Agent-based modelling of social processes — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Use agent-based modelling when a stated theory or mechanism can be represented as interacting agents and a simulation can help explore possible patterns; simulation output is not evidence that real people will behave that way.
- Discourse and conversation analysis — useful and optional
- Experiments, quasi-experiments and causal inference — useful and optional
- Longitudinal, panel and multilevel data — useful and optional
- Piloting questions before fieldwork — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Test whether research questions are understood as intended before fieldwork.
- Programme evaluation and theory of change — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Assess what changed, for whom, why, under what conditions and at what cost.
- Psychometrics and scale construction — useful and optional
- Evidence synthesis: systematic reviews and scoping reviews — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Find, screen, assess and combine existing studies rather than collecting new data by default.
- Use AI cautiously to assist cross-source social-data harmonisation — optional; condition: Experimental optional support for comparing existing datasets. Verify every proposed mapping against source codebooks, populations, measures, periods, and transformation rules.
- Use AI to draft or improve research-data metadata, then curate and verify it — optional; condition: Optional experimental support for drafting or reviewing metadata. A human curator checks every field against the source and repository schema.
- AI-assisted annotation of social-research text, with human validation — optional; condition: Optional experimental support on a justified text-analysis task. Compare model annotations with human-coded examples and retain human interpretive responsibility.
- Validate and document AI use in a research workflow — use only when its stated condition applies; condition: Use only when AI is used in the selected workflow; record the system, version, task, data context, checks, and required disclosure.
- Critical discourse analysis (CDA) — useful and optional
- Exponential-family random graph models (ERGMs) — useful and optional
- LLM-assisted measurement of constructs in social text — useful and optional
- Machine learning for survey nonresponse adjustment — useful and optional

- Define the construct, platform scope and sampling limits of digital traces → Analyse digital text with human validation and error reporting (workflow; For a digital-text classification task, define what the text represents, which platform or records are in scope, and how the sample was selected before making classification claims. Keep a human or non-computational alternative available when classification is not suitable.)
- Define the construct, platform scope and sampling limits of digital traces → Analyse relationships as networks when ties are the evidence (workflow; When using network analysis, define what counts as a node and a relationship, and which records are included, before interpreting network measures. You may learn the design and analysis concepts together.)
- Define the construct, platform scope and sampling limits of digital traces → Predict text only for a defined social question, with validation (workflow; For predictive text work, define the target and the records in scope before fitting a model, then validate it on data that did not train the model. Skip prediction when the question does not call for it.)

8. **Alternatives and order checks:**
- Compare **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts** with **Chi-square tests for categorical counts and association**: For sparse categorical-count tables, choose the exact or justified resampling procedure when the large-sample chi-square approximation is unsuitable. The tests have different reference distributions and sampling assumptions.
- Compare **Barnard's unconditional exact test for 2×2 tables** with **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts**: For independent-binomial 2×2 data, Barnard's unconditional test is an exact alternative when conditioning on both margins is not required. Its an extra unknown value the test has to account for method, variance choice and sampling assumptions differ from Fisher's conditional exact test.
- Compare **Boschloo's unconditional exact test for 2×2 tables** with **Fisher's exact test for categorical counts**: For independent-binomial 2×2 data, Boschloo's unconditional test is an exact alternative based on Fisher's p-value and may be more powerful. State the sampling assumptions and two-sided p-value convention.
- Compare **Wilcoxon rank-sum and signed-rank tests** with **One-sample, independent-samples and paired t-tests**: For a two-group independent or paired contrast, a rank-based procedure may fit the outcome/design better. It targets a rank/distribution contrast and is not a drop-in mean test.
- Compare **Spearman rank correlation** with **Pearson product-moment correlation**: For the same association question, use Spearman for ordinal/rank or monotonic association where Pearson's linear quantitative association is not the target. Related but distinct coefficients answer different questions.
- Compare **Kendall rank correlation** with **Pearson product-moment correlation**: For the same association question, Kendall is a rank-based alternative when concordance, ordinal measurement or tie handling better fits than Pearson's linear association.
- Compare **Kendall rank correlation** with **Spearman rank correlation**: Choose between rank coefficients by the result you set out to estimate, tie pattern, sample and inferential procedure; Kendall tau and Spearman rho are related but not numerically interchangeable.
- Compare **Item response theory (IRT) and differential item functioning** with **Confirmatory factor analysis (CFA)**: IRT and CFA can address measurement structure and comparability through different model frameworks. Choose according to item response format, the result you set out to estimate, sample and assumptions rather than treating scores as equivalent.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SAS/STAT for statistical modelling and survey analysis**: Both document common inferential procedures; choose based on the exact model, access and reproducibility needs.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **Jamovi for introductory quantitative social-science analysis**: Both provide graphical workflows for common statistical procedures; choose based on analysis coverage, reproducibility and access.
- Compare **JASP, menu-based software for statistical analysis** with **SPSS for statistical analysis when the project calls for it**: Both support common introductory tests and models; procedures and licensing differ, so compare the required analysis.
- Compare **MAXQDA for qualitative and mixed-methods analysis** with **ATLAS.ti for qualitative data coding and retrieval**: Both support organizing, coding and retrieving qualitative material; compare workflow and access needs.
- Compare **MAXQDA for qualitative and mixed-methods analysis** with **QualCoder for open-source qualitative analysis**: Both support qualitative coding and retrieval; one is commercial and one is open source.
- Compare **ATLAS.ti for qualitative data coding and retrieval** with **Dedoose for collaborative qualitative and mixed-methods analysis**: Both can support coded qualitative projects; collaboration and mixed-method needs may influence the choice.
- Compare **QualCoder for open-source qualitative analysis** with **Taguette for highlighting and coding documents**: Both support document coding workflows; choose according to collaboration, data types and installation needs.
- Compare **Qualtrics for online survey design and response management** with **ODK for offline-capable field data collection**: Both can implement digital questionnaires; online panel workflows and offline field collection have different strengths.
- Compare **Microsoft Excel** with **R for statistical computing and data analysis**: For this role task, R for statistical computing is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
- Compare **NVivo for organizing and coding substantial qualitative material** with **Taguette for highlighting and coding documents**: For this role task, Taguette is another possible tool route when its workflow, access and task fit better. The tools are alternatives for this use case, not interchangeable for every task.
